POSTSCRIPTUM. 


Born in a country parsonage I, until my twelfth year. 
associated much with peasants, and listened with attentive interest 
to their legends and stories. Amongst these there were two 
especially which made a strong impression upon my childish 
mind: 

One was the legend of the sunken church lying at the 
bottom of the lake where it might be seen deep down in the 
water when it was clear and calm, and whose bells might still 
be heard ringing in the stillness of the evening; the other 
was the story of the treasure-seeker who at sunset, in perfect 
silence, without uttering в word, sought to bring the long burried 
treasure up to the surface. 

І віво laboured and strove for years digging silently, until 
I could bring the treasure forth to the light of day. Неге we 
have it! But it bas long lain hidden and may require a little 
furbishing in coming times, before it can shine in all its glory. 


What induced me with eagerness to begin to work at the 
Jàtaka Book was particularly three utterances I met with: The 
first I found in Spence Hardy’s Manual of Budhism. p. 1, viz. 
„Тһе Singhalese will listen the night through to recitations from 
this work without any apparent weariness, and а great number 
of the Jütakas are familiar even to the women", The second I 
read in Clough’s Singhalese Dictionary under the word Játaka- 


II Postacriptum. 


pota where it says: ,this book is so sacred amongst the Bud- 
dhists that they will offer to it and worship it^. And the third 
I noticed in the Ceylon Friend 1837 where it says: ,The more 
I tbink of Buddha, the more I love him“, When we have read 
the Jataka through no one will wonder at these sentiments, 


In 1849 I bad вігевду commenced transcribing parts of the 
Jüteke, but I did not seriously take it up until I had finished 
my edition of the Dhammapada in 1866. The further I got 
into the book, the clearer I saw its importance, not only in a 
linguistic sense but also from 8 culture-historical point of view, 
and in order to awaken interest for it in the literary world I 
began publishing specimens of it in 1861. Professor Westergaard 
was not at firet in favour of a complete edition, he would have 
preferred an analysis only. Perhaps he thought the undertaking 
beyond my abilities. Later on he altered his opinion and 
supported the work, It was however principally the encourage- 
ment I, from the very beginning, received from Professor A. 
Weber that kept up my courage. And when material failed me, 
it was especially the Rev. Subhiti’s untiring perseverance in 
sending me a paper transcript in parts, and Colonel Duncan’s 
aplendid present of a complete Burmese copy of the Jataka (at 
the instigation of Missionary С. Н, Chard) that made it possible 
for me to finieh my undertaking. 

I now trust that tbe fact will not be overlooked, that I 
have had but little materia] to work from, also that when I 
began the study of Pàli, the language was nearly uncultivated. 
I therefore venture to hope for в mild criticism of this my work,

Paccuppennavatthu.

IV Postacriptum. 


In the Atitavatthu we have the oldest element of the 
Jātaka. The tale of the A. V. is founded on an ancient story, 
originally composed in verse, from which Gotama Buddha quotes 
sometimes single verses sometimes more. We have here an 
entire parallel to many of the Icelandic Saga-works which are 
also built up on the old lays of the Bards. That G. В. himself 
із not the author of these werses, is most clearly seen from the 
later Јаќакев, the verses of which in many places say the same 
as has just been told in prose. It would indeed be ridiculous 
to suppose tbat G. B. should beve exerted himself to express 
in poetry and even in old language what he had juet seid in 
prose, No, he only affirms what he has said in prose by 
quotations from the poem on which bis tale is founded, In 
many instances he does not even convert the old song into prose, 
but leta the tale go on in the very words of the song, only now 
and then putting in some explanatory remarks, see f. ex. II $$}, 
III.889, IV, 504, У,514; VI уту: 220,28-221,19; 485,19-12; 513,17-26; 
548,1-10, 557,9.8 etc. Compare this with what I have said in 
my edition of the Sutta-Nipata p. VII—VIII. It is also worth 
noticing certain recurring phrases which seem to point to our 
having here before us fragments of old popular epic poetry, f. 
ex, kacci vo kusalam VI 5“ 43? сіт. Mahabharata (Calcutta 


edition) ХІІ 18787; see further VI 1: foll 43 35 24 те з»

VIII Postscriptum. 


That is to say: The tales of the Atitavattbu play 
mostly in the northern and western part of India, 
and the tales of the Paccuppannavatthu principally 
in the eastern India. In other words: the Atitavatthu is 
the oldest element of the Jàtaka. This seems especially to be 
evident from the tales in which the Takkasilà is mentioned ав 
a University-town to which young men resorted from Вёгёпазі 
and other easterly cities to study the three Vedas and acquire 
every sort of accomplishment under the guidance of a renowned 
master, (See the Index under Таккавіій), 

`The Paccuppannavatthu and the AtItavatthu together 
with the Veyy&karana and the Samodháüne then make up 
the Jütaks-Atthakatbà (I $3) tbat was translated into 
Singhalese with the exception of the verses which were left in 
the original Pali; and this Singhalese Jataka-Atthakatha has 
later been re-translated into Pāli under the name of Jadta- 
kasse Atthavannanà or Játakaes' Atthavannanà (see I 2. f, 
VI 525, V *1*) which is the Jétaka that now lies before us and 
begins at page 2 of the first volume. 

That the prosaic part of the Atitavattha belongs to the old 
Jütska ie quite clear from the fact that the verses would be 
thoroughly unintelligible without it. 

It may be doubted whether the Introductory Verses 
at p. l, although they are to be found both in C and B, origi- 
nally belong to the Jātakasse Atthevapnnsnü, as they do not 
appear in S which has quite a different Introduction (see vol. IV) 
and only agrees with C and В from the beginning of p. 2: Bà 
panéyem Jàtakass& Atthavannend. If we suppose that they are 
part of tbe J. A. then the author of it has been called upon 
by three persons viz. Atthadassin, Buddhamitta and Buddhadeva 
to write it. 

П. But who is the Author? To be sure, we are told 
by the writer of the Gantba-Varbsa (see Journal of the P. T. 
Soc. 1886 p. 59) that Buddhaghosa is the author, but on this 
you can scarcely rely. It is certain that Buddhaghosa has 
written Visuddbimagga, Sumaügalavilaeinl, Papaücasüdani, Sà- 


Postacriptum. IX 


ratthappeküsint, Manorethapürani and Samantapāsādikā, for this 
clearly appears from the Introductory Verses to these commentaries, 
but that be, besides these voluminous works, should have written 
six others equally large whose author he ie eupposed to be, is very 
incredible, especially if he only etayed three years in Ceylon, 
and was not barely a trenslator, but an independent writer. 

Further, it is not granted either, that the Buddhamitta 
who ів spoken of in the Introductory Verses of the Jàtaka-Attha- 
vappana, is the same with the one that is mentioned in the 
Samantepüsüdika вод the Papajicasidan!, this one being more 
likely to be identical with the Baddhamitta that, according to 
Vasilie's Bouddhisme p. 218 lived „vers la neuf-centiàme année 
après 1а mort de Bouddha“ and consequently was a contemporary 
of Buddhaghosa. Thie last mentioned Buddhamitta, further, 
may be the same with the one who is mentioned as having, in 
the time of Kumáragupte, Samvat 126 erected a Statue of 
Buddha. (Cír. Westergaerd’s Indiske Kejserhuse p. 108, and 
А. Cunningham's Archeol, Survey of India X p. 7). 

III. That the original Pali Atthakatha (see Index) 
= Jütaks-Atthakathaá (I $3) which was translated into 
Singhalese, already has existed as a Book at the time when 
the Safigitikdrakas made the above-mentioned transposition 
(see supra p. III) seems evident. 

IV. The now existing Játakaeea Atthavannana presents 
itself partly as а recast of the Jataka-Atthakathd, an earlier 
arrangement of the stuff being kept formally but having in 
reality heen altered, while several, formerly independent, Jütakas 
have been incorporated in others. We find namely tbat 12 
(110. 111. 112. 170. 192. 350. 364. 452. 471. 500. 508. 517.) 
from their original place have been transferred to 546 Mabhü- 
Ummegga-, 2 (341. 464) to 536 Kunala-, 1 (441) to 546 Vi- 
dbura-, and 1 (470) to 535 Sudbébhojana-; consequently, when 
we subtract these 16 from the current statement of 550, we only 
get 534, but formally there are 547 according to the older 
redaction. In tbis both C and B agree. It would be of interest 
to learn what position the Siamese Jàtska holds in regard to

in Siam.

That the Tipitaka has existed before Gotama Buddha, even 
long before at the time of KondeBüa- Buddha, would also result 
from the Nidánekathà I 52, where we are told that the king 
Vijitàvin mastered the three Pitakaa; likewise from I 1: ** end 
IV У. So it will be understood how we already in the 
Atitavatthu (11137) occasionally meet with the formula: appamano 
Buddho, appamano Dham mo, appamano Samgho, this Buddha 
being of course the Buddha of the time and not Gotama Buddha. 

And perhaps in time we may find out that several of the 
anterior, so-called mythical, Buddhas have been real historical 
persons; one of them at least has proved to be so, since a 
stüpa was erected to him, and Asoka worshipped before 
it and restored it. (See Bühler in the Academy 1895, 27. 
April p. 360). On the whole, I think we must admit that 
such a complicated system ав that of Gotama Buddha's is 
scarcely one man’s work, but must indeed bave bad its fore- 
runners. And that a large old poetic literature in Pali has 
existed before Gotama Buddha is proved by tbe many identical 
‘fragments of verses that recur in different Jatakas, in fact in 
all Pali books, and seem to have been common property at the 
time; compare below Dr. Andersen's List of Parallel Verses.

PREFACE. 


On beginning this Index to the Jataka, four years ago, it 
was first my intention to give en index of both names and 
matter.  Bnt I have altered this plan since the appearance of 
the English translation published by Prof, Cowell. In this 
translation we have not only a short account of the contents of 
the tsles, but a complete index has been held in prospect, when 
the work ie finished. I have therefore particularly confined 
my work to proper names and Gathas. In reality I think that 
indexes to proper names and verses in Pali literature are at 
present of much greater consequence than a new Pali Dictionary. 
Prof. E. Müller's Index of proper names published in J. Р. T. S, 
is, it is true, of great importance, and I have myself derived 
much benefit from it, but it is clear that after a space of nine 
years it cannot longer be satisfactory. I therefore hope that the 
present index will be a coneiderable step forward. My plan has 
been to collect all places where the names occur. How far I 
have been fortunate in this respect, the use of the book will 
show. The names are arranged alphabetically according to the Pali 
Alphabet, and the words of the text itself are everywhere, аз 
far ов possible, employed in the explanations; my own additions 
are marked ( —). The index refers to the six volumes marked 
I—VI, and each place is indicated page and line. Only quota- 
tions from Nidánakathà are separately designated by the letter N. 
In accordance with the now published edition I have tried to 
introduce a further denotion as to whether the quotations are 


п 


XIV Preface, 


from the Jataka-text itself, from the frame story, or the commen- 
tary, whereas the quotations from the commentary are marked 
with (—) enclosing the number of line, whilst quotations from the 
principal text are marked with *. J admit that it would have 
been of interest to have had a special mark for all quotations from 
the verses, but the fear of making the whole too complicated 
has prevented me doing so. Beside the proper names are added 
all names of tales!) or portions of the work and titles of other 
Pali works quoted. Amongst the number of refereuces to pas- 
sages in the Jütaka itself, many of course are incorrect, in such 
cages I have exerted myself to point out the one really meant. 
I will bere merely give an example: Vol. IV 360,24 we have 
the following quotation ,Atthanipüte fucirajátake". There is 
however no tale with this title in the whole Јёќака. The only 
way of fiuding out what is referred to ів the word ,,asadisa- 
dànam", which shortly mentions the subject of the introductory 
tale. Thereby is found in Atthanipüta Adittajdtaka, and atten- 
tion is directed besides to Jat. (499), where it is clear the same 
quotation appears again in the form of ,Atthanipate Sovira- 
jitake“. Neither is there any Sovirsjütake, but when one gets 
accustomed to the different ways in which the tales have come 
by their names in the last redaction of the text, it is easy to 
be seen that the name fovirejütaka must be the right reading, 
ae Adittajütake begins just with the words ,atIte SovIrerat(he". 
Sucira must therefore be a misacript for Sovira, Vidura-jat, 
(Cod. B) is doubtless Vidhüra-jàt. or another name for Dhüme- 
kārijāt. (413). Неге is consequently а coufounding of the Atita- 
vatthu and the Paccuppanne-vatthu. On the other hand the Bir- 
man variation of Sovira IV 401 is Sivira (cfr. ПІ 470 Sivi- 
ratbe B), which also implies that Sucira is а perveraiop. 

The importance of having the words of the Paccupannavatthu, 
pointing out the suhject of the tale, included in the index, may 
thus be seen; І bave therefore unbesitatingly introduced them 
in alphabetical order with the proper names, for it is practical to

The titles of the stories are in most cases formed in either 
of the following ways: 1) the Jütska is called after the hero, 
generally Bodhisatta, but also sometimes after some otber person 
taking part in the tale, 2) the title can be formed according

that we cannot from tbe titles draw any direct conclusion as to 
the origins] form of the tales. I do not think it right to 
assert, that the Jâtakas have originally only existed in metre. 
As these tales have been represented at an early period under 
the form of reliefs, they must bave been widely circulated and 
well known, and have doubtless also been early varrated in 
prose. That great parts of the present redaction are selected 
from longer poems in narrative style, there is little reason to 
doubt, but at the time they were first related ав Jatákas (i. e. 
stories of Buddba’s earlier existences), they may very well have 
been somewhat in their present shape. For oloser examination
